## W999. Store-Level Customer Tile & Flooring Sample Loan & Return Program

| Field | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Trigger** | Customer requests to borrow tile/flooring samples for home evaluation before purchase |
| **Frequency** | ~6,000–8,000 sample loans/month chain-wide (~30–40/store/month) |
| **Volume** | 1–5 samples per loan; average 3 samples |
| **Owner** | Department Supervisor (Tiles & Flooring) |
| **Participants** | Sales Associate (Tile Specialist), Customer, Receiving Clerk (sample returns) |

### Background

Tile and flooring is a high-value, visual purchase where color, texture, and appearance under the customer's actual lighting conditions are critical to satisfaction. Showroom lighting in BuildRight stores (fluorescent/LED) differs significantly from residential lighting (warm LED, natural light through windows), making in-store appearance unreliable. Customers who purchase tiles based on showroom appearance alone frequently experience post-purchase dissatisfaction ("the color looks different in my house"), leading to returns, exchanges, and negative reviews. A sample loan program allows customers to take home small tile samples (10×10 cm or 20×20 cm display swatches) for 7 days to evaluate under actual home lighting and alongside existing décor. This practice is standard in premium tile retailers globally and significantly reduces return rates while increasing conversion (customers who take samples home are 40–60% more likely to purchase than those who don't, per industry benchmarks). The program requires sample inventory management, loan tracking, deposit handling, and return processing.

### Steps

| # | Activity | Role (R) | Role (A) | Duration |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Sample Selection & Loan Request**: Customer selects tile/flooring product(s) from display; Sales Associate provides: (a) display sample swatch (10×10 cm or 20×20 cm tile piece mounted on display board with SKU label); (b) if product has no display sample: Sales Associate cuts a sample from a stock tile using tile cutter (top 30 stores with CNC glass/tile cutter) or provides manufacturer's sample brochure/card; (c) customer selects 1–5 samples for home evaluation; (d) Sales Associate verifies samples are available for loan (not already loaned out) | Sales Associate / Customer | Department Supervisor | 5–8 min |
| 2 | **Loan Registration & Deposit Processing**: Sales Associate registers sample loan in system: (a) customer presents loyalty card or provides name and mobile number for tracking; (b) system records: sample SKU(s), customer ID, loan date, due date (7 calendar days from loan); (c) system generates sample loan receipt with: customer name, sample descriptions, loan date, return deadline, deposit amount; (d) customer pays refundable deposit per sample — PHP 100/sample for ceramic/porcelain, PHP 200/sample for natural stone/premium tiles; deposit collected at POS via separate tender type "Sample Deposit" per W25; (e) system flags sample inventory as "on loan" and prevents other customers from borrowing same sample until returned | Sales Associate / Cashier | Department Supervisor | 5 min |
| 3 | **Customer Home Evaluation Period**: Customer takes samples home for up to 7 days; during this period: (a) customer evaluates tile appearance under natural and artificial lighting at home; (b) customer compares tile with existing wall color, furniture, and cabinetry; (c) customer can return samples earlier if decision is made; (d) system sends automated reminder at Day 5 via SMS if samples not yet returned; (e) at Day 7: if samples not returned, system sends final reminder with grace period extension to Day 10; (f) at Day 10: if samples not returned, deposit is forfeited and customer is charged per sample at full retail price; sample flagged as "sold" in inventory | System | Department Supervisor | Automated |
| 4 | **Sample Return & Deposit Refund**: Customer returns samples: (a) Receiving Clerk or Sales Associate inspects returned samples for damage (cracks, chips, stains from customer use); (b) if undamaged: system processes deposit refund via POS — refunded to original payment method per W101; (c) if damaged: Sales Associate assesses damage severity — minor cosmetic damage (still displayable): full refund; significant damage (not displayable): deposit forfeited to cover sample replacement cost; (d) system updates sample inventory status to "available" for next loan; (e) damaged samples dispositioned per W91 (damaged goods) | Sales Associate / Receiving Clerk | Department Supervisor | 5 min |
| 5 | **Conversion Follow-Up & Purchase Assistance**: When customer returns samples: (a) Sales Associate asks if customer has made a decision; (b) if yes: Sales Associate assists with tile quantity calculation per W963 and order processing; (c) if no: Sales Associate offers additional sample options, alternative tile suggestions, or design consultation per W211; (d) system tracks conversion rate: sample loan → purchase; Category Manager reviews conversion metrics quarterly to optimize sample inventory and program effectiveness | Sales Associate | Department Supervisor | 5–10 min |

### System Touchpoints
- Sample loan registration and tracking module (POS)
- Sample inventory management with loan status tracking
- Refundable deposit processing per W25
- Automated SMS reminder system at Day 5 and Day 7
- Deposit refund processing per W101
- POS quotation and order integration per W542 and W963
- Customer loyalty account integration for loan tracking
- Sample conversion rate analytics dashboard

### Pain Points / Risks
- **Sample inventory management**: Each store maintains 1–3 display samples for the ~20–40 tile SKUs on display-gallery rotation (≈40–120 pieces per store, per the PA-62.1 boards canon), out of ~4,200 chain-level tile SKUs; sample inventory must be tracked separately from sellable inventory; samples must be replaced when damaged or not returned per W91
- **Sample theft/non-return**: Some customers may intentionally keep samples (especially premium natural stone samples which have resale value); deposit system mitigates financial loss; repeat non-returners flagged in system and denied future sample loans
- **Deposit handling**: Refundable deposits create POS accounting complexity (liability until refund or forfeiture); system must track deposits as a liability per W25; monthly reconciliation of outstanding deposits vs. physical samples on loan
- **Hygiene concerns**: Tile samples handled by multiple customers accumulate dirt, fingerprints, and potential contamination; periodic cleaning protocol (weekly wipe-down) maintains display quality; bathroom tile samples must be clearly marked as display-only (not for installation)
- **Space requirements**: Sample storage and display area consumes floor space; Department Supervisor must balance sample coverage (comprehensive selection) with display efficiency (rotating seasonal samples, removing discontinued items)


### Automation Opportunity
- System auto-request of "Sample Selection & Loan Request" (replaces manual Step 1).
- System auto-record of "Loan Registration & Deposit Processing" (replaces manual Step 2).
- System auto-send of "sends automated reminder at Day 5 via SMS if samples not yet returned" (replaces manual Step 3).

### Controls
- CTL-259 (ensure controlled execution — Store Daily Management (PA-07.1).)
- operational: mitigates financial loss; operational: must track deposits as a liability per W25

### Staffing Implication
- **Sales Associate (Tile Specialist)**: Sample loan processing adds ~10–19 hours/month (~0.3–0.6 hours/day) at 30–40 loans/month; absorbed by existing Tiles & Flooring Department Sales Associate
- **Receiving Clerk**: Sample return processing adds ~15–30 min/day; absorbed by existing Receiving Clerk role
- **No incremental headcount**

### Time Estimate
- Sample selection & loan request: 5–8 min
- Loan registration & deposit: 5 min
- Customer evaluation: 7 days (customer time)
- Sample return & refund: 5 min
- Conversion follow-up: 5–10 min
- **Total staff time per loan**: 20–28 min (excluding customer evaluation period)

## W1049. Store-Level Customer Tool Sharpening, Blade Replacement & Small Engine Maintenance Service

| Field | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Trigger** | Customer brings dull/broken tool for sharpening or blade replacement, or requests small engine (lawnmower, chainsaw, generator) tune-up |
| **Frequency** | ~1,500–2,500 service requests/month across all stores |
| **Volume** | 1–3 items per customer visit |
| **Owner** | Tools & Hardware Department Supervisor |
| **Participants** | Sales Associate (Tool Section), Service Technician, Customer |

### Background

BuildRight Depot's tools & hardware categories (hand tools 10%, power tools 5% — combined ~5,250 SKUs) drive significant foot traffic and customer loyalty. Filipino contractors, carpenters, and DIY homeowners rely on hand saws, chisels, planer blades, drill bits, and mower blades that dull with regular use. Professional Filipino carpenters sharpen their chisels and saws weekly. Rather than replacing these tools (cost: PHP 200–2,000 per item), customers increasingly seek sharpening services — a growing value-added service category in Philippine hardware retail. Similarly, small engine maintenance (lawnmowers, chainsaws, portable generators — all carried by BuildRight) requires periodic oil changes, air filter cleaning, and spark plug replacement that customers often cannot perform themselves. This service: (a) drives repeat store visits and incremental merchandise sales (customers buy other items while waiting); (b) extends product lifecycle, reinforcing BuildRight's "partner" brand positioning; (c) generates service revenue (sharpening fees: PHP 50–200 per item; tune-up: PHP 300–800 per engine). This is distinct from W440 (power tool service & repair center operations) which handles complex warranty and non-warranty power tool repairs; this workflow covers the simpler, higher-volume sharpening and basic maintenance services.

### Steps

| # | Activity | Role (R) | Role (A) | Duration |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Service Request Intake & Assessment**: Sales Associate receives customer's tool/equipment and performs initial assessment: (a) item type — hand saw, chisel, plane blade, drill bit, knife, scissors, mower blade, chainsaw chain, other; (b) for small engines: lawnmower, chainsaw, brush cutter, portable generator, pressure washer; (c) condition assessment — dull (normal wear), chipped/damaged blade, broken tooth, bent, rusty; (d) determine serviceability: (i) sharpenable items: hand saws, chisels, plane blades, mower blades, chainsaw chains, knives, scissors — accept for sharpening; (ii) replacement-only items: drill bits <6mm (not economical to sharpen), broken saw blades — sell replacement; (iii) non-serviceable items: severely damaged, counterfeit/unsafe brands — decline with explanation; (e) for small engines: diagnose issue — routine maintenance (oil change, filter clean, spark plug), starting problem, running rough, other — per W440 if complex repair needed; (f) system creates service work order per W544 with customer details, item description, requested service, and estimated completion time | Sales Associate | Tools & Hardware Dept Supervisor | 5–8 min |
| 2 | **Sharpening Execution**: Service Technician performs sharpening at dedicated workbench: (a) **Hand saw sharpening**: clamp saw in vise, use triangular file to sharpen each tooth to original profile and set; verify tooth set uniformity with saw set tool; test cut on scrap wood; (b) **Chisel sharpening**: use bench grinder with water cooling (prevent heat bluing) to restore primary bevel (25° or 30°), then hone on sharpening stone (1000-grit then 6000-grit) for mirror edge; verify sharpness on paper test; (c) **Mower blade sharpening**: remove blade from mower, clamp in vise, use angle grinder to sharpen cutting edge to original angle, balance blade on balancer pin (unbalanced blade causes vibration damage); reinstall; (d) **Chainsaw chain sharpening**: use appropriate round file size per chain pitch, file each cutter at correct depth gauge setting, verify with chain gauge tool; (e) **Drill bit sharpening** (>6mm): use drill bit sharpening jig on bench grinder, verify point angle (118° standard, 135° for harder materials); (f) system logs service completion on work order | Service Technician | Tools & Hardware Dept Supervisor | 10–30 min (varies by item) |
| 3 | **Small Engine Maintenance Execution**: For small engine tune-up requests: (a) drain old engine oil into collection pan — dispose per W82 (hazardous waste); (b) replace with correct oil grade (SAE 30 for most small engines in Philippine climate); (c) clean or replace air filter (foam: wash and oil; paper: replace); (d) replace spark plug with correct model per engine specification; (e) check and clean fuel filter; (f) inspect fuel line for cracks/leaks; (g) check pull-start cord for fraying; (h) test start engine — verify smooth idle and acceleration; (i) if engine fails to start or runs poorly after basic maintenance, escalate to W440 (power tool service & repair center) for diagnostic repair | Service Technician | Tools & Hardware Dept Supervisor | 30–60 min |
| 4 | **Customer Notification & Collection**: (a) system sends SMS notification to customer: "Your tool/equipment service is complete. Please collect at BuildRight [Store Name] Tool Service Counter. Service fee: PHP [amount]"; (b) customer presents service receipt for item collection; (c) customer verifies service quality (test sharpness or engine start at counter); (d) service fee collected at POS per W544 (service work order); (e) if customer declined service after assessment (e.g., opted for replacement instead), original item returned and replacement sale processed; (f) system closes service work order and logs service history linked to original sale transaction (for warranty tools) | Sales Associate / POS Cashier | Tools & Hardware Dept Supervisor | 3–5 min |

### System Touchpoints
- Service work order module (W544) — intake, assessment, execution tracking
- POS service item processing (W544) — service fee collection
- Product master (W252) — replacement blade/drill bit availability
- Inventory management — replacement parts (spark plugs, oil, filters)
- Hazardous waste tracking (W82) — used oil disposal
- Customer notification engine — SMS service completion alerts
- Power tool service center module (W440) — escalation for complex repairs
- Service history logging — linked to original purchase for warranty tools

### Pain Points / Risks
- **Sharpening quality inconsistency**: Sharpening quality depends on technician skill; quality varies without standardized training; implement sharpening skill certification per W518 (cashier training model adapted for service technicians)
- **Customer tool damage liability**: If a tool is damaged during sharpening (e.g., chisel cracked on grinder), customer expects replacement; implement service disclaimer: "BuildRight will exercise due care but is not liable for pre-existing tool conditions (micro-fractures, metal fatigue) that may become apparent during sharpening"
- **Wait time during peak periods**: Saturday mornings generate 3–5× normal service volume; without appointment scheduling, wait times exceed 60 min; consider implementing reservation per W1007 (tool demo reservation adapted for service scheduling)
- **Used oil disposal**: Small engine maintenance generates ~0.5L used oil per service; must be collected and disposed per W82 (hazardous waste) and DENR regulations per W477


### Automation Opportunity
- System auto-count of "counterfeit/unsafe brands — decline with explanation" (replaces manual Step 1).
- System auto-log of "logs service completion on work order" (replaces manual Step 2).
- System auto-capture of "Small Engine Maintenance Execution" (replaces manual Step 3).

### Controls
- CTL-259 (ensure controlled execution — Store Daily Management (PA-07.1).)
- operational: standard operating procedures; system-enforced validation rules

### Staffing Implication
- **Service Technician**: Dedicated role for sharpening and small engine maintenance; at ~8–12 service requests per store per month (per the Frequency), this represents ~2–4 hours/month per store; recommend 1 dedicated Service Technician per 2–3-store cluster with rotating schedule; recommend 1 dedicated Service Technician per 2–3-store cluster with rotating schedule
- **Sales Associate (Tool Section)**: Intake and assessment adds 5–8 min per request; absorbed by existing tool section staff
- **Potential incremental headcount**: If service volume exceeds 12 requests per store per month consistently, consider dedicated Service Technician role (included in the 29-per-store headcount as part of the Stock Associate pool with cross-training per W1042)

### Time Estimate
- Service request intake: 5–8 min
- Sharpening execution: 10–30 min (varies)
- Small engine maintenance: 30–60 min
- Customer notification & collection: 3–5 min
- **Total per item**: 18–43 min for sharpening; 38–73 min for engine maintenance
- **Average throughput**: ~8–12 services per technician per day

## W1051. Store-Level Fire Extinguisher Monthly Inspection, Annual Recharge & BFP Compliance

| Field | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Trigger** | Monthly recurring schedule (1st business day of each month); or annual recharge due date; or after any discharge event |
| **Frequency** | Monthly inspection: 12/year per store; Annual recharge: 1/year per extinguisher |
| **Volume** | 1 inspection round per store per month covering all extinguishers; 200 stores × 15–20 extinguishers per store = ~3,000–4,000 extinguishers chain-wide |
| **Owner** | Store Manager |
| **Participants** | Maintenance/Utility Staff, Store Manager, BFP-Accredited Fire Safety Service Provider |

### Background

The Philippine Bureau of Fire Protection (BFP) under RA 9514 (Fire Code of the Philippines) and its Implementing Rules and Regulations (IRR) mandates that all commercial establishments maintain fire extinguishers in working condition, conduct monthly visual inspections, and perform annual professional servicing (hydrostatic testing and recharge). Each BuildRight Depot store requires approximately 15–20 fire extinguishers depending on store size: (a) ABC dry chemical extinguishers (5 kg minimum) at each department and at the paint mixing station; (b) CO₂ extinguishers near the electrical panel room and IT/server closet; (c) wheeled dry chemical extinguisher (25–50 kg) at the lumber yard and flammable materials storage area; (d) kitchen-type extinguishers in the employee break area. Non-compliance carries penalties per the Fire Code (fines of PHP 10,000–50,000 per violation, or closure order for repeated non-compliance). This workflow is distinct from W758 (store-level fire safety equipment daily inspection) which covers daily visual checks of fire exits and basic equipment presence; this workflow covers the formal monthly inspection with documentation, and the annual professional servicing cycle required by BFP. It is also distinct from W806 (annual fire safety system testing & BFP compliance) which covers building-wide fire alarm, sprinkler, and detection systems; this workflow specifically addresses portable fire extinguishers.

### Steps

| # | Activity | Role (R) | Role (A) | Duration |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Monthly Visual Inspection (Each Extinguisher)**: Maintenance/Utility Staff performs 12-point visual inspection of each fire extinguisher using BFP inspection checklist: (a) extinguisher is in its designated location per store floor plan; (b) access path to extinguisher is unobstructed (minimum 1m clearance); (c) extinguisher signage is visible and legible; (d) pressure gauge needle is in the green zone (for pressurized types); (e) safety pin and tamper seal are intact; (f) hose and nozzle are undamaged and unblocked; (g) extinguisher body has no visible damage, corrosion, or dents; (h) operating instructions on label are legible; (i) maintenance tag is attached and inspection dates are current; (j) extinguisher is within its service life (dry chemical: 5 years from manufacture; CO₂: 10 years); (k) weight is within acceptable range (lift test — if noticeably lighter than labeled weight, may have lost pressure or charge); (l) if any item fails: tag extinguisher as "OUT OF SERVICE," remove from location, place backup extinguisher (stores maintain 10% spare), and create maintenance work order per W47 for immediate service | Maintenance/Utility Staff | Store Manager | 3–5 min per extinguisher × 15–20 extinguishers = 45–100 min per store per month |
| 2 | **Inspection Documentation & Digital Logging**: (a) for each extinguisher: Maintenance/Utility Staff scans extinguisher barcode/QR code (linked to asset master per W399), selects pass/fail for each of the 12 inspection points, and takes photo of pressure gauge; (b) system auto-populates: extinguisher ID, location, type (ABC dry chem/CO₂/other), capacity, manufacturer, last service date, next service due date; (c) system stores inspection record with timestamp, inspector name, and photo evidence; (d) if all 12 points pass: system marks extinguisher as "Inspected — Compliant" with next monthly inspection date; (e) if any point fails: system creates maintenance work order (W47) and flags for immediate corrective action; (f) system generates store-level monthly compliance summary: total extinguishers, passed, failed, corrective actions taken; (g) Store Manager reviews and signs off on monthly summary | Maintenance/Utility Staff / System | Store Manager | 5–10 min (documentation per store) |
| 3 | **Annual Professional Servicing & Recharge**: Once per year (per extinguisher service anniversary date), Store Manager coordinates with BFP-accredited fire safety service provider: (a) service provider performs on-site professional servicing: (i) discharge test (verify extinguisher agent discharge correctly); (ii) internal inspection of cylinder and valve assembly; (iii) hydrostatic pressure test for CO₂ and dry chemical cylinders per BFP schedule; (iv) refill/recharge extinguisher agent to specified weight/pressure; (v) replace safety pin and tamper seal; (vi) attach new BFP-accredited service tag with service date and provider license number; (b) any extinguisher failing hydrostatic test or found to have cylinder damage is condemned and must be replaced — Store Manager initiates capex request per W21 or operating expense per W136 (indirect procurement); (c) service provider issues BFP-compliant service certificate for the store; (d) system updates fire extinguisher asset records per W399 with new service date, next service due, and service provider details; (e) service certificate uploaded to compliance document repository per W255 (electronic document storage) | Store Manager / BFP-Accredited Service Provider | Store Manager | 2–4 hours per store per year |
| 4 | **BFP Inspection Readiness & Audit Trail**: (a) system maintains perpetual compliance dashboard per store: each extinguisher's inspection history, service history, and compliance status; (b) if BFP conducts unannounced fire safety inspection per W476 (BFP FSIC management): Store Manager presents: (i) current month's inspection records; (ii) annual service certificates for all extinguishers; (iii) extinguisher location floor plan; (iv) maintenance and corrective action records; (c) system generates BFP inspection readiness report on demand; (d) any compliance gaps (missed monthly inspections, overdue annual service) are flagged in the compliance dashboard per W506 (unified regulatory compliance calendar) and escalated to Store Manager and Regional Operations Manager | Store Manager / System | Regional Operations Manager | 10–15 min (report generation) |

### System Touchpoints
- Fire extinguisher asset master (W399) — extinguisher ID, type, location, service history
- Inspection checklist module — 12-point visual inspection with barcode/QR scan
- Photo capture and storage — pressure gauge photo evidence
- Maintenance work order system (W47) — failed inspection corrective actions
- Compliance calendar (W506) — monthly inspection schedule, annual service due dates
- BFP FSIC compliance module (W476) — inspection readiness
- Electronic document storage (W255) — service certificates
- Indirect procurement module (W136) — replacement extinguisher purchasing
- Capex module (W21) — replacement for condemned units

### Pain Points / Risks
- **Inspection fatigue**: Monthly 12-point inspection of 15–20 extinguishers is repetitive; risk of pencil-whipping (marking all items as compliant without actual inspection); mitigate with photo evidence requirement for pressure gauge reading
- **Spare extinguisher inventory**: Stores must maintain 10% spare extinguishers (~2 units) to immediately replace condemned or discharged units; spare inventory tracked per INV-001
- **Service provider scheduling**: BFP-accredited service providers serve many establishments; annual service scheduling must be done 30–60 days in advance to secure preferred dates; system sends 60-day advance reminder per W506
- **Cost of non-compliance**: BFP can issue closure order for expired/unserviced extinguishers; estimated annual extinguisher servicing cost per store: PHP 10,000–16,000 (PHP 500–800 per unit × 20 units) — allocated to store operating budget per W489


### Automation Opportunity
- System auto-validate of "Monthly Visual Inspection" (replaces manual Step 1).
- System auto-scan of "scans extinguisher barcode/QR" (replaces manual Step 2).
- System auto-record of "records per W399 with new service date, next service due, and service provider…" (replaces manual Step 3).

### Controls
- CTL-259 (ensure controlled execution — Store Daily Management (PA-07.1).)
- operational: extinguisher service docket with 60/30-day lead reminders; out-of-service units tagged and swapped same-day

### Staffing Implication
- **Maintenance/Utility Staff (1 per store)**: Monthly inspection: 45–100 min; absorbed into existing maintenance schedule
- **Store Manager**: Monthly review and sign-off: 10–15 min; annual service coordination: 2–4 hours
- **No permanent incremental headcount**

### Time Estimate
- Monthly visual inspection: 45–100 min per store per month (3–5 min per extinguisher)
- Documentation & logging: 5–10 min per store per month
- Annual professional servicing: 2–4 hours per store per year (coordinated with service provider)
- BFP readiness reporting: 10–15 min on demand
- **Monthly time burden**: ~60–120 min per store

## W1070. Store-Level Customer Building Permit Documentation Package Preparation Assistance

| Field | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Trigger** | Customer requests assistance in preparing or organizing documentation required for local government building permit application for residential or small commercial construction |
| **Frequency** | ~2,500–4,000 customer interactions/month across all stores (LGU building permit is required for most construction projects in the Philippines) |
| **Volume** | 1 documentation package per customer |
| **Owner** | Pro Desk / Trade Counter Supervisor |
| **Participants** | Sales Associate (Pro Desk), Customer |

### Background

Obtaining a building permit in the Philippines is a multi-step bureaucratic process involving the Local Government Unit (LGU) Office of the Building Official (OBO). The National Building Code of the Philippines (PD 1096) and its Implementing Rules and Regulations (IRR) mandate specific documentation for building permit applications. For a typical residential or small commercial construction project, the required documents include: (a) duly accomplished building permit application form (LGU-specific); (b) lot plan and survey plan (prepared by licensed geodetic engineer); (c) building plans / architectural plans (prepared by licensed architect); (d) structural plans and calculations (prepared by licensed civil engineer); (e) electrical plans (prepared by licensed professional electrical engineer); (f) plumbing plans (prepared by licensed master plumber); (g) sanitary / mechanical plans (if applicable); (h) fire safety evaluation and compliance clearance per W476; (i) barangay clearance; (j) tax declaration and real property tax clearance; (k) DLL (Daily Log of Labor) and construction safety health program per DOLE; (l) contractor's license and PCAB (Philippine Contractors Accreditation Board) registration; (m) structural and geotechnical soil report (if required); (n) environmental compliance certificate (ECC) or certificate of non-coverage from DENR (if applicable); (o) Homeowners' Association (HOA) clearance (if in a subdivision); (p) fire safety inspection certificate from BFP per W476. BuildRight Depot's pro desk frequently encounters customers (especially DIY homeowners and small contractors) who are unfamiliar with the documentation requirements and whose projects are delayed by weeks or months due to incomplete permit applications. This workflow provides a documentation checklist and organizational assistance service — not a permit facilitation or expediting service — to help customers understand and prepare their building permit requirements, reducing project delays and increasing customer goodwill. It is distinct from W1056 (construction permit advisory) which provides general guidance; this workflow specifically addresses the documentation package preparation and organization.

### Steps

| # | Activity | Role (R) | Role (A) | Duration |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Project Classification & Permit Requirement Determination**: Sales Associate determines permit requirements: (a) project type: new residential construction (1-story, 2-story, 3+ story), residential renovation/extension, new commercial construction, commercial renovation, fence/wall construction, demolition; (b) total floor area and number of floors; (c) project location: LGU name, barangay, subdivision (if applicable); (d) estimated construction cost (for fee computation); (e) system cross-references project parameters against PD 1096 requirements and LGU-specific checklist (system maintains a database of major LGU building permit requirements — initially covering BuildRight's 200 store locations across 150+ LGUs); (f) system determines which professional sign-offs are required: architect, civil engineer, electrical engineer, master plumber, sanitary engineer, fire safety engineer; (g) system determines which LGU offices must be visited: OBO (Building Official), barangay hall, BFP fire station, DENR (if applicable), HOA (if applicable) | Sales Associate / System | Pro Desk Supervisor | 5–8 min |
| 2 | **Documentation Checklist Generation**: System generates a customized permit documentation checklist: (a) system produces a detailed checklist specific to the project type and LGU, organized by submission order; (b) each checklist item includes: document name, issuing authority/professional, required copies (typically 3–5 sets), required format (blueprint, notarized, authenticated), estimated time to obtain (1 day to 6 weeks), and estimated cost of obtaining (professional fees, LGU fees); (c) typical professional fee ranges provided for guidance: architect (PHP 5–15% of construction cost), structural engineer (PHP 3–8%), electrical engineer (PHP 2–5%), master plumber (PHP 2–5%), geodetic engineer for lot plan (PHP 5,000–20,000); (d) LGU fee estimate computed per LGU fee schedule (typically 0.5–1.0% of estimated construction cost for building permit fee + ancillary fees); (e) system highlights critical-path documents (those with longest lead times, typically structural plans and geodetic survey: 2–4 weeks each) and recommends starting those first; (f) system flags documents that require notarization and provides list of nearby notary publics | System | Sales Associate | 2–3 min (automated) |
| 3 | **Professional Referral & Document Organizer**: (a) system offers referral to licensed professionals from BuildRight's partner network per W904: architects, civil engineers, electrical engineers, master plumbers, geodetic engineers — organized by service area (city/municipality); (b) system generates a printable "permit document organizer" folder cover sheet with: project name, applicant name, checklist with checkboxes for tracking completion, submission deadline tracker, LGU office visit log (date, office, person contacted, status); (c) for BuildRight pro/trade account customers: system offers to store digital copies of submitted permit documents in the customer's project file per W255 (electronic document storage) for future reference (e.g., renovation permits, FSIC renewals); (d) system provides LGU office addresses, operating hours, and contact numbers for the relevant OBO, barangay hall, BFP station, and DENR office | Sales Associate | Pro Desk Supervisor | 3–5 min |
| 4 | **Material List Integration & Project Kickstart**: (a) if customer has not yet purchased construction materials, system offers to generate material estimates per relevant workflows: W1044 (concrete), W1064 (electrical panel), W1068 (earthquake-resistant materials), W1069 (flood-resistant materials), W983 (roofing), W989 (plumbing layout), W1043 (paint), W991 (electrical circuit design); (b) system links material estimates to the permit documentation — the structural plans will specify material grades (rebar grade, concrete strength, steel specifications) that must match the materials purchased; (c) for B2B/trade customers converting to project quote per W162, system attaches permit documentation checklist to the project file; (d) system offers notification service: customer can opt in to receive reminders at key permit milestones (e.g., "Your building permit application has been pending for 3 weeks — have you followed up with OBO?") | Sales Associate | Pro Desk Supervisor | 3–5 min |

### System Touchpoints
- LGU building permit requirements database (PD 1096 + LGU-specific rules for 150+ LGUs)
- Professional fee estimation engine
- LGU fee computation engine
- Contractor/professional referral database (W904)
- Electronic document storage integration (W255)
- Material estimation workflow integration (W1044, W1064, W1068, etc.)
- B2B quotation module (W162)
- Loyalty account integration (W17)
- Notification/reminders engine

### Pain Points / Risks
- **LGU variability**: Each of the 150+ LGUs where BuildRight operates may have slightly different requirements; the LGU database must be regularly updated per W657 (regulatory change management); initial coverage should focus on top 50 LGUs by store count and expand incrementally
- **Not a permit facilitation service**: BuildRight does NOT facilitate, expedite, or bribe for permits; the service is strictly informational and organizational; anti-bribery compliance per W656 (ABAC program) must be reinforced with all pro desk staff
- **Professional liability**: BuildRight does not prepare engineering or architectural plans; referrals to licensed professionals are independent third parties and BuildRight is not liable for their work product
- **Corruption risk**: Building permit processes in some LGUs are associated with corruption/facilitation fees; BuildRight staff must never advise customers to pay facilitation fees or "fixers"; mitigated by standard operating procedures and system-enforced validation rules


### Automation Opportunity
- System auto-validate of "Project Classification & Permit Requirement Determination" (replaces manual Step 1).
- System auto-generate of "blueprint, notarized, authenticated" (replaces manual Step 2).
- System auto-log of "Professional Referral & Document Organizer" (replaces manual Step 3).

### Controls
- CTL-259 (ensure controlled execution — Store Daily Management (PA-07.1).)
- operational: standard operating procedures; system-enforced validation rules

### Staffing Implication
- **Sales Associate (Pro Desk)**: Permit documentation assistance adds 10–15 minutes per customer; at ~3–5 requests per store per week (per the Frequency), this represents ~0.5–1.2 hours/week; absorbed by existing Pro Desk staff (1 CSR + 1 supervisor per store)
- **No permanent incremental headcount**

### Time Estimate
- Project classification: 5–8 min
- Checklist generation: 2–3 min (automated)
- Professional referral & organizer: 3–5 min
- Material integration: 3–5 min
- **Total per consultation**: 13–21 min

## W1085. Store-Level Appliance Display Model Lifecycle & Periodic Refresh Management

| Field | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Trigger** | Scheduled quarterly appliance display rotation, new model introduction by vendor, display model reaching maximum display age threshold (typically 90–120 days), or vendor-initiated display refresh program |
| **Frequency** | Quarterly rotation + ad-hoc vendor refreshes (~4–6 refresh cycles per year per store) |
| **Volume** | 15–25 appliance display units per store per rotation cycle (appliances are 5% of SKUs, ~1,750 items, with ~20–30 units on active display per store at any time) |
| **Owner** | Department Supervisor (Appliances Section) |
| **Participants** | Sales Associate (Appliance Section), Receiving Clerk, Store Manager, Merchandising Category Manager (HQ) |

### Background

Home appliances (fans, air conditioners, water heaters, kitchen appliances) represent ~5% of BuildRight's active SKUs (~1,750 items) and a significant portion of revenue per unit. Unlike tools or hardware, appliances are high-value items (PHP 3,000–50,000+) that customers want to see, touch, and operate before purchasing. Each BuildRight store maintains approximately 20–30 appliance display units across categories: ceiling fans, stand fans, wall fans, window-type air conditioners, split-type air conditioners, electric and LPG stoves, water heaters (tank and tankless), refrigerators (selected stores), and washing machines (selected stores). Display models have a finite lifecycle: they accumulate cosmetic damage, become outdated when newer models launch, consume floor space that could be allocated to faster-moving new products, and lose visual appeal. However, display models also represent significant locked-up capital — a single split-type AC unit on display may cost PHP 25,000–40,000. The display model lifecycle workflow ensures that: (a) displays are refreshed at optimal intervals to maintain visual appeal and showcase current models; (b) retired display units are monetized through demo-unit sales at discounted prices (per W524); (c) vendors participate in display costs through co-op funding per W513; (d) display inventory is accurately tracked and valued in the ERP. This is distinct from W97 (sample & demo inventory management) which covers the broader policy; this workflow addresses the operational execution of appliance display rotation and the specific retail challenges of large appliance units.

### Steps

| # | Activity | Role (R) | Role (A) | Duration |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Display Age & Condition Assessment**: (a) system generates quarterly display aging report for each store: listing all appliance display units by SKU, date placed on display, days on display, and original cost; (b) Department Supervisor physically inspects each display unit: cosmetic condition (scratches, dents, discoloration), functional condition (powered on, all features working, remote control present), cleanliness, shelf label accuracy (correct model number, price, specifications), and visual merchandising compliance (per planogram per W314); (c) Supervisor rates each unit: Grade A (like-new, suitable for continued display), Grade B (minor cosmetic wear, can continue display with discount note), Grade C (significant wear, functional but visually impaired, candidate for immediate retirement); (d) assessment results entered into ERP against each display unit's serial number | Department Supervisor | Store Manager | 2–3 hrs per assessment (quarterly) |
| 2 | **Rotation Decision & Vendor Coordination**: (a) for units exceeding 90-day display age: system flags for rotation review; (b) Category Manager (HQ) reviews flags and determines rotation plan: (i) extend display period for strong-selling models with Grade A condition; (ii) initiate vendor-funded display refresh for models where vendor has co-op display program per W513; (iii) retire and sell display unit as demo model per W524; (iv) transfer to another store in the region where the model is not yet on display per W22 (stock transfers); (c) for vendor-funded refresh: Category Manager contacts vendor for new display unit shipment and return/replacement of old unit; (d) new model introduction: when a vendor launches a new appliance model, Category Manager schedules phased rollout across stores (not all 200 stores receive simultaneously; priority by region and store tier); (e) rotation schedule communicated to each affected store via ERP task notification | Category Manager (HQ) | VP Merchandising | 4–6 hrs per quarterly cycle |
| 3 | **Display Unit Retirement & Demo Sale Preparation**: For units designated for retirement: (a) Department Supervisor powers down, disconnects, and cleans unit; (b) Receiving Clerk performs condition report: documents all cosmetic defects with photos, confirms all accessories present (remote, manual, mounting hardware, warranty card), and verifies functional test; (c) system reclassifies inventory from "Display Asset" to "Demo/Open-Box Saleable" per W549 (damaged & open-box discount processing); (d) system calculates demo sale price: original SRP × discount factor based on condition grade (Grade A: 10–15% off SRP, Grade B: 20–30% off SRP, Grade C: 35–50% off SRP); (e) demo price approved by Store Manager for Grade A/B discounts; Grade C discounts require Category Manager approval via ERP workflow; (f) unit placed on demo sale display area with prominent "Display Model Sale" signage, condition disclosure, and modified warranty terms (typically: 6-month store warranty instead of manufacturer's full warranty) | Department Supervisor / Receiving Clerk | Store Manager | 30–45 min per unit |
| 4 | **New Display Unit Receipt & Setup**: (a) Receiving Clerk receives new display unit from vendor or DC transfer; (b) unboxes and performs initial inspection for shipping damage; (c) Department Supervisor sets up unit on display floor: mounts/positions per planogram (W314), connects to power (ensuring proper electrical load per W985 if air conditioner), programs demo mode (continuous operation mode for display), attaches price tag and feature callout cards, updates shelf label in ERP per W181; (d) system updates display tracking record: new SKU, serial number, date placed on display, expected rotation date (90 days), display location (aisle, bay, shelf position); (e) for air conditioners: Store Manager ensures dedicated electrical circuit for display to avoid tripping store breakers; (f) old display unit's serial number is deactivated from display tracking and activated in demo sale inventory | Department Supervisor / Receiving Clerk | Store Manager | 45–60 min per unit |
| 5 | **Display Inventory Reconciliation & Financial Reporting**: (a) monthly: system generates display inventory valuation report per store showing total capital locked in display models, aging profile, and demo sale revenue; (b) quarterly: Category Manager reviews display ROI — sales generated per display unit vs. capital employed; (c) vendor co-op display funding reconciliation: system matches vendor display invoices against co-op advertising budget per W513; (d) demo sale revenue tracked separately in P&L per store; (e) inventory accuracy: display units included in cycle count per W6 (cycle counting) — verified by serial number during monthly count | System / Category Manager | VP Merchandising | Monthly report: 30 min; Quarterly review: 2–3 hrs |

### System Touchpoints
- Display inventory tracking module (serialized unit tracking with display status flag)
- Inventory reclassification module (display asset → demo saleable)
- Planogram & space planning module (W314)
- Vendor co-op advertising & display funding module (W513)
- POS demo/open-box sale processing (W549)
- Stock transfer module (W22)
- Cycle counting module (W6)
- Shelf label & price tag system (W181)
- Store-level P&L reporting (W67)

### Pain Points / Risks
- **Capital lock-up**: 20–30 display units per store × PHP 10,000 average cost = PHP 200,000–300,000 per store in display capital; across 200 stores = PHP 40M–60M total display inventory; must be managed tightly
- **Display damage risk**: Appliance display units are handled by hundreds of customers per week; damage rates are high (~15–20% of units accumulate significant cosmetic wear within 90 days); mitigated by CTL-259 (controlled execution — Store Daily Management (PA-07.1))
- **Electrical load constraints**: Running multiple air conditioners and appliances on display simultaneously draws significant power; store electrical circuits must be designed to handle display load without tripping breakers
- **Vendor display funding disputes**: Vendors may dispute co-op display charges if rotation timing doesn't align with their new model launch schedule; clear contractual terms required per W62 (vendor contract lifecycle)


### Automation Opportunity
- System auto-capture of "entered into ERP against each display unit's serial number" (replaces manual Step 1).
- System auto-review of "Rotation Decision & Vendor Coordination" (replaces manual Step 2).
- System auto-document of "documents all cosmetic defects with photos, confirms all accessories present" (replaces manual Step 3).

### Controls
- CTL-259 (ensure controlled execution — Store Daily Management (PA-07.1).)
- operational: per W62 (vendor contract lifecycle)

### Staffing Implication
- **Department Supervisor (Appliances)**: Display rotation adds ~2–3 hours per quarterly cycle for assessment and setup; absorbed by existing role
- **Receiving Clerk**: Additional receipt and setup work adds ~30–45 min per unit; at ~5–8 units rotated per quarter out of the 20–30 on display per store, this is ~3–6 hours/quarter; absorbed by existing role
- **No permanent incremental headcount**

### Time Estimate
- Quarterly assessment: 2–3 hrs per store
- Rotation decision & vendor coordination: 4–6 hrs (HQ, quarterly)
- Retirement & demo sale prep: 30–45 min per unit
- New display setup: 45–60 min per unit
- Monthly reconciliation: 30 min
- **Total per quarterly cycle**: ~8–12 hrs per store + 4–6 hrs HQ

## W1091. Store-Level Customer Fence & Gate Installation Service Partner Coordination

| Field | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Trigger** | Customer purchases fencing materials (GI sheets, hollow blocks, steel bars, chain-link fence, gate hardware) and requests installation service, or Sales Associate proactively offers installation coordination during fence material estimation per W987 |
| **Frequency** | ~2,500–4,000 coordination requests/month across all stores (fencing is a common project for Filipino homeowners securing their property perimeter) |
| **Volume** | 1 fence/gate installation project coordination per request |
| **Owner** | Trade Counter / Pro Desk Supervisor |
| **Participants** | Sales Associate (Trade Counter / Lumber & Building Materials), Installation Service Partner (accredited fence contractor), Customer |

### Background

Fencing is one of the most common construction projects in the Philippines — nearly every Filipino homeowner needs a perimeter fence for security and property delineation. BuildRight sells all the materials: GI corrugated sheets (for the most common Filipino fence type — "tangkal" or GI sheet fence on steel frame), hollow blocks and cement (for CHB perimeter walls), steel bars (rebar for reinforced concrete fence posts), chain-link fence (for commercial properties and agricultural use), pre-fabricated steel fence panels, and gate hardware (hinges, latches, locks, wheels for sliding gates). However, most homeowners and many small contractors lack the specialized skills for proper fence and gate installation: (a) fence post foundation depth and concrete mix (minimum 0.8m depth in Philippine soil for 2m fence height); (b) steel frame welding for GI sheet fence (weld quality affects longevity in Philippine weather — salt air corrosion in coastal areas, typhoon wind resistance); (c) gate fabrication and hanging (sliding vs. swing, wheel track installation, post bracing for heavy steel gates); (d) compliance with LGU fence height regulations (typically 2m maximum for front fence, 2.5m for side/rear per local zoning ordinance). This workflow extends W987 (fence material estimation) by adding the installation service coordination layer. It integrates with W138 (home installation services management) and W600 (service contractor accreditation) for the partner management aspects.

### Steps

| # | Activity | Role (R) | Role (A) | Duration |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Project Scope & Site Assessment**: (a) Sales Associate reviews the fence/gate project details from W987 material estimation: fence type (GI sheet, CHB wall, chain-link, steel panel), total fence length (meters), fence height, number and type of gates (swing or sliding, pedestrian or vehicle), and soil condition (clay, sandy, rocky — affects post foundation depth); (b) Associate confirms customer's installation service interest and gathers: site address, site accessibility (narrow alley access may limit equipment), power availability for welding (or generator requirement), and target completion timeline; (c) for complex projects (perimeter > 50m, or includes vehicle sliding gate): Associate recommends an on-site visit by the installation partner before quotation | Sales Associate | Trade Counter Supervisor | 5–8 min |
| 2 | **Installation Partner Selection & Quotation**: (a) system queries accredited fence/gate installation partners in the customer's area per W600 (service contractor accreditation); (b) system displays available partners with: partner name, accreditation level, completed projects count, LGU business permit status, customer rating, service area coverage, and current availability (lead time to start); (c) system auto-selects top-rated available partner or offers customer a choice of 2–3 options; (d) system generates installation cost estimate based on: fence type × linear meter rate, gate fabrication and installation × unit rate, foundation/concrete work × volume rate, welding labor × hour estimate, and equipment rental (if generator or scaffolding needed); (e) installation estimate ranges: GI sheet fence: PHP 800–1,500/linear meter (labor only); CHB perimeter wall: PHP 1,500–3,000/linear meter (labor + concrete); sliding vehicle gate (3m width): PHP 8,000–20,000 (fabrication + installation); (f) combined estimate: materials (from W987) + installation labor = total project cost | System | Trade Counter Supervisor | 3–5 min (automated) |
| 3 | **Partner Assignment & Scheduling**: (a) customer approves combined materials + installation estimate; (b) system assigns project to selected installation partner and sends notification with: project scope, site address, customer contact, material list, scheduled material delivery date, and target installation start date; (c) system schedules material delivery to customer site: fence posts, GI sheets or CHB, cement, sand, steel bars, and gate materials delivered via BuildRight's home delivery service per W19 or W668 (store-level home delivery coordination); (d) installation partner confirms schedule within 24 hours; (e) customer receives confirmation via SMS/email with: installation partner name and contact, scheduled start date, estimated completion date, and BuildRight customer service hotline for issues | System / Trade Counter Supervisor | Trade Counter Supervisor | 5–8 min |
| 4 | **Installation Execution & Quality Monitoring**: (a) installation partner arrives on-site on scheduled date; (b) partner verifies material delivery completeness against the material list; (c) partner executes installation: (i) fence post hole digging and concrete pouring; (ii) steel frame fabrication (welding for GI sheet fence) or CHB wall laying; (iii) fence material attachment (GI sheet screwing, chain-link tensioning, or steel panel bolting); (iv) gate fabrication (if custom), hanging, and hardware installation; (v) clean-up; (d) customer inspects completed work; (e) partner provides installation warranty certificate (typically 1-year workmanship warranty); (f) system sends customer satisfaction survey via SMS; (g) for any issues: customer contacts BuildRight customer service per W41 (complaint resolution); BuildRight coordinates with installation partner for warranty rework per W795 (service customer complaint & warranty claim) | Installation Partner / Customer | Trade Counter Supervisor | Installation: 1–5 days depending on scope; Monitoring: automated |
| 5 | **Partner Settlement & Performance Tracking**: (a) upon customer acceptance (step 4d), system triggers payment to installation partner: labor cost per estimate minus 10% retention released after 30-day defect-free period; (b) for trade account customers: installation cost added to monthly billing per W8; for B2C customers: payment collected at POS or via payment link before installation begins; (c) BuildRight earns coordination fee: 8–12% of installation labor cost; (d) system updates partner performance record: on-time start rate, on-time completion rate, customer satisfaction score, warranty rework rate, and safety incident count; (e) quarterly: Trade Counter Supervisor reviews partner performance; underperforming partners placed on probation or replaced per W600 accreditation criteria | System / Trade Counter Supervisor | Finance Manager | Per-project settlement: automated; Quarterly review: 2–3 hrs |

### System Touchpoints
- Service contractor accreditation module (W600)
- Partner availability & scheduling module
- Material estimation module (W987 integration)
- Home delivery scheduling module (W19 / W668)
- POS invoicing module (W542)
- Trade account billing module (W8)
- Customer notification engine (SMS/email)
- Customer complaint module (W41)
- Service warranty module (W795)
- Partner performance dashboard

### Pain Points / Risks
- **Partner quality variability**: Fence installation quality varies significantly between accredited partners; steel frame welding quality is the most common deficiency (poor welds lead to fence failure during typhoons)
- **Material theft risk**: Delivered fence materials left on-site before installation begins are vulnerable to theft; timing of material delivery and installation start must be tightly coordinated; mitigated by the must allow for estimate adjustment with customer approval
- **Site condition surprises**: Underground obstructions (rocks, old foundations, buried pipes) discovered during post hole digging increase labor cost and timeline; system must allow for estimate adjustment with customer approval
- **LGU permit compliance**: Some LGUs require a fence construction permit, particularly for perimeter walls exceeding 1.8m; the installation partner must verify and obtain permits, but BuildRight shares reputational risk for non-compliance


### Automation Opportunity
- System auto-review of "reviews the fence/gate project details from W987 material estimation" (replaces manual Step 1).
- System auto-count of "count, LGU business permit status, customer rating, service area coverage, and…" (replaces manual Step 2).
- System auto-send of "Partner Assignment & Scheduling" (replaces manual Step 3).

### Controls
- CTL-259 (ensure controlled execution — Store Daily Management (PA-07.1).)
- operational: must allow for estimate adjustment with customer approval

### Staffing Implication
- **Sales Associate (Trade Counter)**: Installation coordination adds 10–15 minutes per project; at ~3–5 projects per store per week (per the Frequency), this represents ~0.5–1.2 hours/week — absorbed by existing Trade Counter staff
- **No permanent incremental headcount** — coordination fee revenue covers the labor allocation

### Time Estimate
- Project scope & assessment: 5–8 min
- Partner selection & quotation: 3–5 min
- Scheduling: 5–8 min
- Installation monitoring: automated
- Settlement: automated
- **Total per coordination**: 13–21 min active staff time

## W1106. Store-Level Community Building Materials Donation & LGU Partnership

| Field | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Trigger** | LGU (barangay/municipality/city) formal donation request, community disaster relief event (W428), annual CSR budget allocation, or corporate social responsibility initiative activation |
| **Frequency** | ~2–5 donation requests per store per month; ~400–1,000 total/month across chain |
| **Volume** | 1 donation request per event |
| **Owner** | Store Manager |
| **Participants** | Store Manager, Department Supervisor (Lumber/Building Materials), CMO (Marketing/CSR), VP Legal & Compliance, Receiving Clerk |

### Background

BuildRight Depot, as the largest hardware and home improvement retailer in its Philippine market, regularly receives requests from LGUs (barangays, municipalities, cities), public schools, churches, and community organizations for building material donations. These requests range from: (a) small barangay requests — 10 bags of cement and 20 hollow blocks for a community basketball court repair; (b) medium school/hospital requests — paint, lumber, and electrical supplies for classroom renovation or barangay health center improvement; (c) large disaster relief donations — roofing sheets, nails, lumber, and cement for typhoon-affected families (complementing W428); (d) annual CSR commitments — corporate pledged donations to partner communities. These donations serve dual purposes: community goodwill (essential for store operations in Philippine communities where barangay captain and local government support is critical for business permits, traffic management, and security) and brand visibility. However, donations must be properly controlled to prevent abuse, ensure equitable distribution, maintain inventory accuracy, and comply with tax requirements (BIR donation documentation, tax deductibility). This workflow manages the end-to-end donation process from request to delivery, complementing W444 (community solicitation & donation processing) which handles corporate-level donation policy, and W135 (CSR program execution) which manages the marketing and reporting aspects.

### Steps

| # | Activity | Role (R) | Role (A) | Duration |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Donation Request Receipt & Validation**: (a) Store Manager receives donation request from LGU/community organization — format may be formal letter (with LGU letterhead and barangay captain signature), email, or in-person request; (b) requestor must provide: requesting organization name, authorized signatory, purpose of donation, specific materials and quantities requested, target completion date, and benefit to community; (c) Store Manager validates: (i) request is from a legitimate organization (LGU, registered NGO, public school, religious institution with SEC registration); (ii) request is within BuildRight's CSR focus areas (community infrastructure, education, disaster relief, environmental sustainability); (iii) request does not involve political campaign materials or partisan activities; (iv) requesting entity has not received a donation from the same store in the past 6 months (to prevent repeated requests from the same barangay) | Store Manager | Store Manager | 15–30 min |
| 2 | **Donation Value Assessment & Approval Routing**: (a) Store Manager assesses donation value at SRP (standard retail price): (i) **< PHP 5,000**: Store Manager approval authority; items from SLOB/clearance inventory preferred (W220); (ii) **PHP 5,001–25,000**: Regional Manager approval required; (iii) **PHP 25,001–100,000**: VP Legal & CMO approval required; (iv) **> PHP 100,000**: CEO/CFO approval required via corporate CSR committee; (b) system checks inventory availability at store and nearby stores; (c) preference given to: (i) SLOB/clearance items that would otherwise be written off, (ii) slow-moving seasonal items, (iii) display/demo units; (d) Store Manager prepares donation authorization form in ERP with: requesting entity details, items and quantities, estimated value, justification, and proposed delivery method | Store Manager | Approver per threshold | 20–30 min |
| 3 | **Inventory Deduction & Donation Documentation**: (a) upon approval, system generates a zero-value sales order (donation order type) with special pricing = PHP 0.00; (b) inventory deducted from store stock at WAC (weighted average cost); (c) system generates: (i) donation delivery receipt (DDR) — itemized list of donated materials with quantities and SRP values; (ii) BIR-compliant donation certificate — for tax deductibility, includes BuildRight's TIN, donee's TIN, description and fair market value of goods; (iii) acknowledgment receipt — for donee's signature upon receipt; (d) cost of donated goods recorded as CSR expense in GL: debit CSR Expense, credit Inventory; (e) for tax purposes: donation expense is deductible up to a certain percentage of taxable income per NIRC (National Internal Revenue Code); Finance tracks cumulative annual donation expense vs. cap | System / Store Manager | Store Manager | 15–20 min |
| 4 | **Donation Staging & Handover**: (a) Stock Associate picks donated items from inventory and stages at receiving dock; (b) Receiving Clerk loads donated materials onto requestor's vehicle or BuildRight delivery truck (if delivery is approved); (c) requestor's authorized representative signs acknowledgment receipt and presents valid ID; (d) photos taken of donated materials and handover for CSR documentation and social media (coordinated with Marketing per W135); (e) Store Manager sends handover confirmation to regional office | Stock Associate / Receiving Clerk | Store Manager | 20–45 min |
| 5 | **Post-Donation Reporting & Community Impact Tracking**: (a) Store Manager logs donation completion in CSR module with photos and acknowledgment receipt scan; (b) monthly CSR donation report generated per store and consolidated at corporate: total donations by value, by category, by beneficiary type; (c) Marketing team (W135) follows up with beneficiary for community impact story and social media content; (d) annual CSR report (W694) includes donation statistics; (e) LGU relationship management: Store Manager follows up with barangay captain to ensure materials were used as intended and maintains positive relationship for business permit renewal (W54) and community support | Store Manager / Marketing | CMO | 15 min per donation |

### System Touchpoints
- Zero-value donation order type in ERP (special sales order with zero pricing)
- Inventory deduction at WAC — standard inventory management module
- CSR/donation tracking module — request, approval, documentation, reporting
- BIR donation certificate generation — tax compliance module
- GL posting — CSR expense and inventory credit
- Photo/document attachment module — handover documentation
- Social media content management (W142) — impact stories
- Community relationship tracker — LGU contact management

### Pain Points / Risks
- **Donation request abuse**: Some barangay officials may make repeated or inflated requests; the 6-month cooldown per entity and tiered approval thresholds mitigate this
- **Political sensitivity**: Donations must not be perceived as supporting specific political candidates; all donations must go to registered organizations, not individuals
- **Inventory impact**: Large donations can deplete store inventory of in-demand items; preference for SLOB/clearance stock minimizes this
- **Tax documentation gaps**: If BIR donation certificates are not properly generated, BuildRight cannot claim the tax deduction; system must enforce documentation completeness
- **Community expectations**: Regular donations may create an expectation of ongoing support; stores must clearly communicate that donations are discretionary and subject to budget availability


### Automation Opportunity
- System auto-validate of "Donation Request Receipt & Validation" (replaces manual Step 1).
- System auto-generate of "prepares donation authorization form in ERP" (replaces manual Step 2).
- System auto-record of "recorded as CSR expense in GL" (replaces manual Step 3).

### Controls
- CTL-259 (ensure controlled execution — Store Daily Management (PA-07.1).)
- operational: must enforce documentation completeness

### Staffing Implication
- **Store Manager**: Donation processing adds ~2–3 hours/week during peak periods (post-typhoon, holiday season)
- **Stock Associate / Receiving Clerk**: Picking and loading donated items adds ~30–60 min per donation event
- **No permanent incremental headcount**

### Time Estimate
- Request validation: 15–30 min
- Approval routing: 20–30 min
- Documentation: 15–20 min
- Staging & handover: 20–45 min
- Reporting: 15 min
- **Total per donation**: 85–140 min

## W1116. Store-Level Customer Complaint Trend Analysis & Systemic Issue Escalation

| Field | Detail |
|---|---|
| **Trigger** | Weekly complaint trend review schedule, or threshold breach alert (complaint volume spike) |
| **Frequency** | Weekly (trend analysis); real-time (threshold alerts) |
| **Volume** | ~10–15 customer complaints per store per month; ~2,000–3,000/month across chain (per W41) |
| **Owner** | Store Manager |
| **Participants** | Store Manager, Department Supervisors, Customer Service Representative, Category Manager (escalated), Quality Assurance |

### Background

BuildRight Depot receives customer complaints through multiple channels: in-store (customer service counter), phone (call center W259), email, social media (W142), and online reviews (W510). While individual complaints are resolved per W41 (customer complaint resolution), there is a critical need for systematic trend analysis to identify: (a) recurring product quality issues (e.g., a specific brand of cement bags consistently arriving damaged); (b) service quality patterns (e.g., long checkout wait times on weekends at specific stores); (c) systemic process failures (e.g., BOPIS orders frequently not ready within 4-hour SLA); (d) emerging product safety concerns (e.g., multiple customers reporting that a specific brand of extension cord overheats); (e) staff behavior patterns (e.g., a specific cashier consistently generating complaints about incorrect change). Without systematic trend analysis, individual complaints are resolved in isolation, and root causes remain unaddressed. This workflow manages the weekly analytical review and escalation process at the store level, complementing W507 (customer complaint root cause analysis & systemic improvement) which handles the corporate-level analysis, and W597 (complaint escalation matrix) which manages individual case escalation.

### Steps

| # | Activity | Role (R) | Role (A) | Duration |
|---|---|---|---|---|
| 1 | **Complaint Data Aggregation & Classification**: (a) system automatically aggregates all complaint records for the past 7 days from all channels: in-store (POS customer service log), call center (ticketing system W258), email, social media, and online reviews; (b) each complaint is classified by: (i) **Category**: Product Quality, Pricing Error, Stock Availability, Service Quality, Delivery Issue, Returns/Exchange Issue, Staff Behavior, Store Cleanliness, Safety Concern, Other; (ii) **Product**: SKU, brand, category; (iii) **Severity**: Low (minor inconvenience), Medium (financial impact ≤ PHP 5,000), High (financial impact > PHP 5,000 or safety concern), Critical (injury, legal threat, media exposure); (iv) **Resolution Status**: Resolved, Pending, Escalated, Unresolved; (c) system generates weekly complaint dashboard: total complaints by category, top 10 complained-about SKUs, top 10 complained-about issues, complaints per department, complaints per channel, comparison to prior week and rolling 4-week average | System | Store Manager | automated |
| 2 | **Trend Identification & Threshold Breach Detection**: (a) system performs automated trend detection: (i) **Week-over-week increase**: any complaint category increasing > 25% WoW triggers alert; (ii) **SKU-specific spike**: any SKU with ≥ 5 complaints in a week (vs. typical 0–1) triggers product quality alert; (iii) **Department-specific spike**: any department with complaint rate > 2× its rolling average triggers staff/service alert; (iv) **Safety complaints**: any complaint mentioning "injury," "fire," "electric shock," "chemical burn," or "structural failure" auto-escalates to Critical severity regardless of volume; (b) Store Manager reviews dashboard and system-identified trends; (c) Store Manager adds qualitative context: (i) were there external factors (typhoon, holiday rush, construction nearby)? (ii) is a new product in its launch period (expected higher complaints)? (iii) were there staffing issues (absences, new hires)? | System / Store Manager | Store Manager | 30–45 min/week |
| 3 | **Root Cause Investigation & Local Resolution**: (a) for store-level root causes, Store Manager assigns Department Supervisor to investigate: (i) **Product Quality issues**: inspect affected items, check receiving records, photograph defects; (ii) **Service Quality issues**: review CCTV footage, observe staff interactions, check POS transaction logs; (iii) **Stock Availability issues**: review replenishment records, check DC fill rates, verify demand forecast accuracy; (iv) **Safety Concerns**: immediately quarantine affected product per W875 (product recall inventory quarantine), inspect remaining stock, photograph hazards; (b) Department Supervisor proposes corrective action: (i) staff retraining; (ii) process adjustment; (iii) product return to vendor (W88); (iv) store layout or signage change; (v) product quarantine or removal from shelf; (c) Store Manager approves and tracks implementation | Department Supervisor | Store Manager | 1–3 hours/week |
| 4 | **Systemic Issue Escalation to Corporate**: (a) if root cause investigation reveals a systemic issue beyond store-level resolution, Store Manager escalates: (i) **Product quality (multi-store)**: escalate to Category Manager with evidence — photos, complaint data, batch/lot numbers; Category Manager initiates vendor quality CAPA (W110) or product recall assessment (W873); (ii) **Process failure (multi-store)**: escalate to COO with documentation — similar complaints at other stores suggest a systemic process issue requiring corporate policy change; (iii) **Safety concern (any)**: escalate immediately to VP Legal, COO, and Quality Assurance — potential product recall (W873) or legal liability (W285); (iv) **IT system issue**: escalate to IT helpdesk (W48) — e.g., POS pricing errors causing widespread customer complaints; (b) escalation documented in complaint tracking system with expected resolution timeline and responsible corporate owner | Store Manager | COO (for systemic issues) | 30–60 min per escalation |
| 5 | **Monthly Complaint Performance Report & Continuous Improvement**: (a) system generates monthly complaint performance scorecard per store: (i) total complaints per 1,000 transactions (benchmark: < 2 complaints per 1,000 transactions); (ii) average resolution time (target: ≤ 24 hours for Low/Medium, ≤ 4 hours for High, ≤ 1 hour for Critical); (iii) complaint-to-compliment ratio; (iv) repeat complaint rate (same customer complaining > 1 time in 30 days); (v) Net Promoter Score (NPS) correlation; (b) scorecard included in monthly store performance review (W67); (c) trends inform: (i) Category Manager assortment decisions (W679 — delist high-complaint items); (ii) HR training needs (W51 — targeted staff training); (iii) Operations improvement initiatives (process redesign); (iv) Vendor negotiations (W44 — complaint data as leverage for vendor improvement) | System / Store Manager | COO | automated + 1 hour/week review |

### System Touchpoints
- Customer complaint ticketing system (W258) — multi-channel complaint capture
- POS customer service log — in-store complaint recording
- Social media monitoring (W142) — complaint capture from Facebook, Instagram, Twitter
- Online review module (W510) — review-based complaints
- Complaint classification and analytics engine — automated categorization
- Threshold alert system — automated spike detection
- Product quarantine module (W875) — safety-related product holds
- Vendor quality CAPA module (W110) — vendor escalation
- Product recall assessment module (W873) — safety escalation
- Store performance dashboard (W665) — complaint metrics integration
- BI analytics (W880) — trend analysis and reporting

### Pain Points / Risks
- **Complaint under-reporting**: Filipino cultural tendency to avoid confrontation may mean many dissatisfied customers do not formally complain; actual dissatisfaction may be 3–5× higher than recorded complaints
- **Social media velocity**: A single viral complaint on Facebook or TikTok can generate hundreds of negative comments within hours; real-time social media monitoring is essential
- **Classification inconsistency**: If Department Supervisors classify complaints differently across stores, trend analysis will be unreliable; standardized complaint taxonomy is critical
- **Investigation resource constraints**: Store Managers have limited time for root cause investigation; high-priority issues (safety, legal) must be prioritized over lower-impact complaints
- **Escalation accountability**: Corporate-level escalations may languish without a clear SLA for corporate response; Store Manager must track and follow up on unresolved escalations


### Automation Opportunity
- System auto-capture of "Complaint Data Aggregation & Classification" (replaces manual Step 1).
- System auto-review of "reviews dashboard and system-identified trends" (replaces manual Step 2).
- System auto-log of "Root Cause Investigation & Local Resolution" (replaces manual Step 3).

### Controls
- CTL-259 (ensure controlled execution — Store Daily Management (PA-07.1).)
- operational: Store Manager review and approval gate

### Staffing Implication
- **Store Manager**: Weekly complaint review adds 30–45 minutes; escalation adds 30–60 minutes per event
- **Department Supervisor**: Root cause investigation adds 1–3 hours/week
- **No permanent incremental headcount** — analytical tools automate the data aggregation, freeing staff time for investigation and resolution

### Time Estimate
- Data aggregation: automated
- Trend review: 30–45 min/week
- Root cause investigation: 1–3 hours/week
- Escalation: 30–60 min per event
- Monthly reporting: automated + 1 hour/week
- **Total per store per week**: ~3–5 hours (Store Manager + Department Supervisor)
